ChatGPT powiedzia³:
Great question — and the answer depends on how you launch and architect these Local Reward Tokens (LRTs) + Global Reward Token (GRT).
Let’s break it down clearly so you understand all options and their consequences.

? If someone already has reward tokens, can they trade them?
YES — but only if liquidity exists.
Reward tokens (GRT, LRTs) can be traded immediately if the following is true:
    • There is some liquidity on a DEX
    • Or you create a simple P2P swap system
    • Or you create a swap widget inside Kasparex
If no liquidity + no P2P = tokens are technically tradeable but have no market.

? Option 1: Launch LRTs + GRT without liquidity
(They are NOT tradeable yet)
Users can:
    • Receive rewards
    • Collect them
    • See their balances
    • Use them inside Kasparex (points, boosts, unlocks)
But they cannot trade, unless:
    • you add LP
    • or build a P2P system
This creates:
    • early accumulation
    • zero selling
    • zero dumping pressure
    • clean ecosystem start
This is actually GOOD for the beginning.

? Option 2: Provide automatic liquidity (recommended long-term)
This means:
    • GRT has a liquidity pool (KAS–GRT)
    • Every LRT has its own LRT–KAS pool
    • Liquidity grows automatically via:
        ? % of fees
        ? % of emissions
        ? protocol-owned liquidity
This is the most decentralized + scalable approach.
BUT:
    • Requires creating pools
    • Requires initial liquidity
    • Requires DEX integration
    • Includes price discovery (volatility)

? Option 3: Build Kasparex P2P Swap (super simple)
A built-in swap page where:
    • User A sends LRT ? User B
    • User B sends KAS (or GRT) ? User A
    • Smart contract locks both until both sides sign
    • Or atomic escrow-style contracts
This is extremely useful because:
? No liquidity needed
? No volatility
? Users can still trade
? Perfect for gated micro-tokens
? Works even for very small LRTs
This is how many platforms start with “internal tokens” before they list anywhere.

? Option 4: Build a Kasparex OTC Desk
Even simpler:
    • User posts:
“Selling 50,000 QUEST for 500 KAS”
    • Another user accepts
    • Smart contract executes atomic swap
This becomes a marketplace for microtokens.
ZERO LP needed.

? Which approach is best for you?
SHORT-TERM (launch stage)
Use:
? No liquidity
? Rewards only
? Internal utility
? P2P Trading interface
This ensures:
    • No dumps
    • No price chaos
    • Users accumulate
    • LRT identity forms first
    • GRT gains presence
MEDIUM-TERM (after 1–2 months)
Add:
? Global liquidity pool for GRT
? Optional liquidity pools for popular LRTs
? Auto-LP from fees
LONG-TERM
Turn everything into:
    • Full DEX trading
    • Token pairs
    • Liquidity incentives
    • Cross-token utility
    • Leaderboards
    • Seasonal emissions

? Recommended Strategy (best for your ecosystem)
I would use this hybrid:
Phase 1 – Accumulation (No Trading)
    • GRT + LRT are awarded
    • Users accumulate
    • UI shows “Swap Coming Soon”
    • Fees flow into treasuries
    • No selling pressure
Duration: 2–4 weeks

Phase 2 – P2P swaps enabled
Users can:
    • Swap
    • Offer
    • Trade
    • Negotiate
    • Gift
    • Auction
No LP yet.
Duration: 1–2 months

Phase 3 – GRT gets its first liquidity pool
    • KAS/GRT pair
    • Controlled depth
    • Price stabilizes
    • Ecosystem token becomes “real”
Duration: ongoing

Not all LRTs need LP.
Only the ones with:
    • high usage

or

Here are clean, short, punchy versions, ultra-simple and easy to understand:

Kasparex dApp Marketplace is the first Proof Of Utility system on Kaspa.
Every click, action, or transaction is recorded on chain as Proof of Utility.

1. Render (Web Services / Cron / Background jobs)
Pricing: https://render.com/pricing#plans
? Pros
    • Very easy to deploy Node.js backends.
    • Free tier available.
    • Autoscaling for bigger instances.
    • Persistent storage options.
    • Good for long-running servers and APIs.
? Cons
    • Much more expensive at scale than Cloudflare
    • You pay for instance size, not per request.
Meaning: if 1000 or 100000 users hit your service, you pay the same fixed cost.
But a marketplace like yours will not need long-running servers for everything.
Most queries are:
    • Get token info
    • Get NFT metadata
    • Fetch lists
    • Quick dApp calls
    • Serve JSON
    • Fetch from IPFS
    • Query explorer API
    • Handle wallet requests
    • Authentication
These are perfect for serverless (Workers), not a server instance.
Typical Render cost for your project
    • 1–2 backend services (Node): 7 USD each minimal
    • Production instance for high throughput: 25–85 USD/mo
    • Maybe extra caching or background worker: 7–25 USD/mo
Monthly cost realistically: 45–120 USD just for backend.

2. Cloudflare Workers (Serverless compute)
Pricing:
https://developers.cloudflare.com/workers/platform/pricing/
? Pros
    • CHEAPEST at scale
    • No servers to maintain.
    • Billions of requests at extremely low cost.
    • Includes KV, D1, Durable Objects, R2 storage.
    • Global edge network = lowest latency.
    • Perfect for metadata delivery, static JSON, token lookups.
    • Free tier is very generous.
    • You can run your entire Kasparex Marketplace on Workers.
Costs
    • Free tier: 100k requests/day
    • Paid: 0.50–$0.15 per million requests depending on plan
    • KV storage very cheap
    • R2 (S3-like storage) extremely cheap
    • Zero cold starts
Why it’s perfect for Kasparex
Kasparex Marketplace uses:
    • Fast requests for dApp metadata
    • Token list fetches
    • IPFS caching
    • API proxying to explorers
    • Wallet connection backend
    • User activity logs
    • Serving dApp JSON
    • Light computation
Cloudflare Workers is designed exactly for this.
Typical monthly cost for Kasparex Marketplace
    • With 1+ million requests monthly: ~1 USD
    • Heavy usage: 10M requests: 5–10 USD
    • Extreme usage: 100M requests: 30–40 USD
    • Storage: R2 = cents
Monthly cost realistically: 1–20 USD at large scale.
This is 10× to 30× cheaper than Render.

a) Vercel Serverless / Edge Functions
    • Very good
    • Same concept as Cloudflare Workers
    • More expensive

    • Strong for frontend, not best for backend-heavy marketplace

    • Similar to Vercel
    • Cheaper, but fewer edge locations
    • Slower than Cloudflare

    • Ultra cheap
    • Ultra flexible
    • But you must maintain servers
    • Not good for gigantic scaling spikes
    • Not serverless

? NODES

Now, the question about running the website on Nodes run by users, to reduce costs etc. Is this even possible, what do you think about that?

Short answer: Yes, it’s possible.
Realistic answer: You need a hybrid design because a full user-run node network is extremely hard to maintain for a dynamic dApp marketplace.
Best answer for Kasparex: Yes, but only for certain parts of the system.
Let me break it down in a very clear way so you can understand exactly what is possible, what is not, and what is the smartest approach for Kasparex and future vProgs.

1. The Big Idea
You want to reduce costs on Vercel, avoid centralization, and let the community run nodes that help host parts of the Kasparex ecosystem.
This is basically the idea of decentralized hosting.
But there are three kinds of web workloads:
A. Static
HTML, CSS, JS, images, metadata
? These can be hosted on IPFS, Storacha, user nodes.
B. Dynamic
API requests, DB queries, auth, wallet actions, user dashboards
? Harder to decentralize.
C. Heavy logic
Search engines, filtering, analytics, real-time updates
? Nearly impossible for user nodes unless you build a full peer-to-peer architecture (expensive, complex, slow).
So the question becomes:
Which parts of Kasparex can be decentralized without breaking functionality?

2. YES, You Can Run Many Parts on User Nodes
Here are the layers that CAN be decentralized right now:
1. Static Frontend
Kasparex Marketplace pages, dApp widget HTML + JS, images, descriptions
? Store on IPFS or Storacha
? Users pin them
? Your Vercel costs drop massively.
2. Metadata Files
dApp definitions, layouts, UI schemas, widget settings
? Perfect for IPFS, replicated by community nodes.
3. Smart Contract Data
All state changes live on Kaspa L1/L2, so this part is already decentralized.
4. Open APIs with P2P Mirror Nodes
People can run local replicas of your public API:
    • dApp list
    • Token list
    • Contract addresses
    • Static JSON configs
This reduces load on your main server.
5. Content Delivering from User Nodes
Users run:
    • IPFS node
    • Storacha node
    • Gateway node
Your website can load assets from:
    • Their node
    • Or fallback ? your node
This is how Brave, ENS, IPFS gateways, Fleek work.

3. What CANNOT Be Decentralized (yet)
1. User Dashboards
Because they need:
    • sessions
    • database queries
    • auth
2. Real-time dApp interactions on L2
Contract calls must come from a backend or direct user wallet.
If you want user nodes to help process backend functions, you’d need a full P2P DB layer.
Not realistic.
3. Developer uploads
Building dApps requires:
    • build tools
    • security checks
    • version management
You cannot let random nodes publish files to your live marketplace.

4. The Smart Solution for Kasparex
You should build a hybrid model (95% decentralized, 5% centralized):
? Static + metadata ? decentralized
Via IPFS / Storacha
Community nodes pin everything.
? dApps themselves ? decentralized
Smart contracts + widgets = self-contained.
? Platform logic (search, dashboard, auth) ? centralized
Light server footprint
Minimal Vercel cost.
? Caching / mirroring ? user nodes
Optional mirror servers maintained by the community.

5. The Result
Benefits:
    • You reduce Vercel costs dramatically.
    • Your marketplace becomes censorship-resistant.
    • Users can volunteer storage + bandwidth.
    • Kasparex becomes a hybrid dApp ecosystem.
    • You introduce a future incentive layer (GRID, LFT1, etc.) that rewards node operators.
Challenges:
    • Updates must propagate to IPFS.
    • Some frontend caching logic must handle fallback.

6. Advanced Future Option (Very Powerful)
You could introduce:
KEX Prime Nodes (Kasparex Prime Network)
A small node clients can run (like a lightweight daemon):
    • Syncs dApp metadata
    • Pins dApps from IPFS
    • Mirrors static assets
    • Relays analytics in anonymized form
    • Stores contract ABI files
    • Fetches L2 chain index cache
This is not heavy like running a Kaspa node. It’s more like running a mini-reactor that keeps the marketplace decentralized.
This could be rewarded in GRID or Local Reward Tokens (LRT).
This is huge for marketing because:
Kasparex becomes not only a dApp marketplace but a community-powered infrastructure layer.

7. Final Answer
YES, you can run your website using user-run nodes — but ONLY the static & metadata layers.
Dynamic backend still needs a small central server.
The best solution is a hybrid model with community nodes mirroring + pinning content.
This gives you:
    • 80 percent cost reduction
    • Resilience
    • Decentralization
    • Branding power
    • Future reward system integration

1. Kasparex Light Node – Architecture Overview
This is NOT a blockchain node.
This is a helper node that stores static assets and metadata to reduce your Vercel usage.
What it does:
    • Pins Kasparex files to IPFS/Storacha
    • Mirrors dApp metadata
    • Mirrors widget HTML/JS
    • Keeps local cache of:
        ? dApp list
        ? Token list
        ? Contract list

    • Sends pings to prove uptime (future GRID rewards)
What it does NOT do:
    • Doesn’t query private data
    • Doesn’t handle wallets
    • Doesn’t replace your backend
    • Doesn’t run compute-heavy tasks
Technology:
    • Node.js
    • IPFS HTTP client
    • Storacha client
    • Tiny Express server (optional)
    • Simple JSON cache

2. Node Types
To make it simple, we start with two types:
A. Light Node (Basic)
Features:
    • IPFS pinning
    • Download static assets
    • Mirror dApp metadata
    • Local JSON cache
    • 0.1 CPU usage
    • Runs on any machine + Raspberry Pi
B. Mirror Node (Advanced)
Everything Light Node does, plus:
    • Hosts a local read-only API:
        ? /dapps
        ? /tokens
        ? /contracts
        ? /widgets
    • Serves static assets
    • Can be used as a fallback by Kasparex frontend
Later:
    • Rewarded with GRID based on uptime.

        ? uptime

5. How Kasparex Uses User Nodes (Hybrid Model)
1. Kasparex publishes:
    • dApp metadata (JSON)
    • Widget HTML/JS
    • Token metadata
    • Contract addresses
2. This metadata is uploaded to:
    • IPFS
    • Storacha
    • CDN (fallback)
    • Light Node bundle
3. User nodes download & pin the data.
4. Kasparex frontend loads from:
Priority:
    1. Storacha
    2. IPFS public gateway
    3. Local user node (optional)
    4. Kasparex Vercel (fallback)
This reduces your hosting costs by 70 to 90 percent.

2. Reward System Logic Module (for GRID Nodes)
This is a backend-side module that lives in your Kasparex API (not on the node itself).
It:
    • Receives pings from GRID Nodes
    • Stores stats per epoch (e.g. daily)
    • Calculates GRID rewards for each node
    • Applies multipliers for Mirror Nodes, uptime, data served, etc.
2.1. Suggested file layout
In your API repo:
src/
  Prime/
    Prime-rewards.js
    Prime-storage.js
    Prime-validator.js
    Prime-controller.js
Below is a compact but complete Prime-rewards.js you can adapt.
2.2. Prime-rewards.js
// src/Prime/Prime-rewards.js

/**
 * GRID Node reward engine for Kasparex Prime
 *
 * Terminology:
 * - Epoch: reward period (e.g. 1 day)
 * - GRID: Global Reward Token (points or tokens)
 * - Role: "light" or "mirror"
 */

const DEFAULT_CONFIG = {
  epochDurationHours: 24,
  baseRewardPerEpoch: 50,          // base GRID units
  maxRewardPerEpoch: 500,          // hard cap for anti-abuse
  minUptimeHoursToQualify: 2,      // minimum uptime in epoch
  weights: {
    uptime: 0.5,       // 50% of score
    pinnedCids: 0.2,   // 20%
    apiRequests: 0.2,  // 20%
    reputation: 0.1    // 10%
  },
  roleMultipliers: {
    light: 1.0,
    mirror: 2.0
  }
};

/**
 * nodeStats example:
 * {
 *   nodeId: "node_abc",
 *   role: "light" | "mirror",
 *   uptimeHours: 18,
 *   pinnedCids: 120,
 *   apiRequestsServed: 2300,
 *   reputationScore: 0.8, // 0..1
 *   epochId: "2025-11-15"
 * }
 */
function calculateGridReward(nodeStats, config = DEFAULT_CONFIG) {
  const cfg = { ...DEFAULT_CONFIG, ...config };

  // Sanity
  if (!nodeStats || !nodeStats.nodeId) {
    throw new Error("nodeStats.nodeId is required");
  }

  const {
    uptimeHours,
    pinnedCids,
    apiRequestsServed,
    reputationScore,
    role
  } = nodeStats;

  // 1. Qualification check
  if (uptimeHours < cfg.minUptimeHoursToQualify) {
    return {
      nodeId: nodeStats.nodeId,
      epochId: nodeStats.epochId,
      qualified: false,
      gridReward: 0,
      reason: "Not enough uptime in this epoch"
    };
  }

  // 2. Normalize metrics (simple heuristics, tweak later)
  const uptimeNorm = clamp(uptimeHours / cfg.epochDurationHours, 0, 1);
  const pinnedNorm = clamp(pinnedCids / 200, 0, 1);          // 200 CIDs ~= full score
  const apiNorm = clamp(apiRequestsServed / 5000, 0, 1);     // 5000 reqs ~= full score
  const repNorm = clamp(reputationScore, 0, 1);

  // 3. Weighted score
  const score =
    uptimeNorm   * cfg.weights.uptime +
    pinnedNorm   * cfg.weights.pinnedCids +
    apiNorm      * cfg.weights.apiRequests +
    repNorm      * cfg.weights.reputation;

  // 4. Raw reward before role multiplier
  let reward = cfg.baseRewardPerEpoch * score;

  // 5. Role multiplier (mirror nodes get more)
  const roleMult = cfg.roleMultipliers[role] || 1.0;
  reward *= roleMult;

  // 6. Anti-abuse cap
  if (reward > cfg.maxRewardPerEpoch) {
    reward = cfg.maxRewardPerEpoch;
  }

  // Round to integer or 2 decimals depending on how GRID is defined
  const gridReward = Math.round(reward);

  return {
    nodeId: nodeStats.nodeId,
    epochId: nodeStats.epochId,
    qualified: true,
    gridReward,
    breakdown: {
      uptimeNorm,
      pinnedNorm,
      apiNorm,
      repNorm,
      roleMult,
      score
    }
  };
}

function clamp(v, min, max) {
  return Math.max(min, Math.min(v, max));
}

module.exports = {
  calculateGridReward,
  DEFAULT_CONFIG
};
2.3. Example usage (cron job)
// src/Prime/Prime-cron.js
const { calculateGridReward } = require("./Prime-rewards");
const { getAllNodeStatsForEpoch, saveEpochReward } = require("./Prime-storage");

async function runEpochRewards(epochId) {
  const nodes = await getAllNodeStatsForEpoch(epochId);

  for (const nodeStats of nodes) {
    const result = calculateGridReward(nodeStats);

    await saveEpochReward({
      nodeId: result.nodeId,
      epochId: result.epochId,
      gridReward: result.gridReward,
      qualified: result.qualified,
      breakdown: result.breakdown
    });
  }
}

module.exports = { runEpochRewards };
Later you can:
    • Start with gridReward as points only.
    • When GRID token exists on Kaspa, map 1 point = 1 GRID.

4. Public Page Explaining Light Nodes (for Kasparex site)
Below is copy you can paste into Wix and adjust.
I’ll keep it simple, clean, and story friendly.

Title
Run a GRID Node · Join the Kasparex Prime
Intro
Kasparex Prime is a community powered layer that keeps the Kasparex dApp Marketplace online, fast and censorship resistant.
By running a GRID Node, you help:
    • Pin and mirror dApp files
    • Keep metadata available even if central servers are down
    • Strengthen the Krex ecosystem for all builders and token holders
In return, GRID Nodes can earn GRID rewards for their support.

What is a GRID Node?
A GRID Node is a lightweight helper node that you run on your own computer or server.
It is not a Kaspa node and it does not require heavy hardware.
A GRID Node:
    • Downloads dApp metadata from Kasparex
    • Pins and mirrors files via IPFS / Storacha
    • Optionally exposes a small API to serve cached data
    • Regularly pings the Kasparex Core to report uptime and stats
Think of it as a small relay that keeps the Kasparex signal alive across the network.

Why run a GRID Node?
1. Support decentralization
You help move Kasparex away from a single hosting provider and towards a distributed Prime of community nodes.
2. Help other projects
Your node helps keep other projects dApps, widgets and tools alive, not just KREX.
3. Potential rewards
The Kasparex Prime includes a reward system where active GRID Nodes can earn GRID points or tokens based on:
    • Uptime
    • Pinned data
    • Requests served
    • Long term reputation
4. Low hardware requirements
A GRID Node is light.
You can run it on:
    • A home PC
    • A small VPS
    • Even a Raspberry Pi (advanced users)

Node Types
Kasparex Prime supports two levels:
1. Light GRID Node
    • Pins IPFS / Storacha CIDs
    • Caches dApp metadata locally
    • Periodically syncs with Kasparex Core
    • Ideal for regular community members
2. Mirror GRID Node
    • Does everything a Light Node does
    • Also exposes a small HTTP API (read only)
    • Can be used as a fallback data source for the Kasparex frontend
    • Ideal for power users and partners

How to run a GRID Node (simple overview)
This is the user friendly version. You can link to technical docs for full steps.
Step 1: Install Node.js
Download Node.js (LTS version) from the official site, install it on your machine.
Step 2: Download the GRID Node package
Clone or download the Kasparex Prime GRID Node repo (link to GitHub).
Step 3: Install dependencies
Open a terminal in the node folder and run:
npm install
Step 4: Start the node
Run:
npm start
The node will:
    • Sync base data from Kasparex
    • Start pinning configured CIDs
    • Report its status in the terminal
Step 5: Optional – run as a background service
Advanced users can use tools like pm2 or system services to keep the node running persistently.

How rewards work (high level)
The reward engine works in epochs (for example, once per day).
During each epoch, your node sends small status pings to Kasparex Core.
At the end of the epoch, the system calculates your GRID reward based on:
    • Uptime within that epoch
    • How many dApp files you helped pin or mirror
    • How many requests your node served (for Mirror Nodes)
    • Your long term reputation score
If you meet minimum uptime, you qualify for a reward.
Mirror Nodes receive a higher multiplier because they serve more traffic.
Details about tokenomics, exact rates and distribution will be published separately.

Is it safe?
A GRID Node:
    • Does not access your wallets or private keys
    • Does not require your Kasparex account password
    • Only handles public dApp metadata and static files
You always control when to start or stop your node.

Who is this for?
    • KREX and Kasparex supporters who want to help the ecosystem
    • Technical users who like running nodes
    • Partners or projects who want extra resilience for their dApps
If you want to do more than just hold a token and click like on posts, running a GRID Node is for you.

Roadmap for GRID Nodes

        ? Basic pinning and metadata mirroring

    2. v2
        ? Full Mirror Node mode

1. EXTENDED Reward Module
Add Local Reward Tokens (LRT) + KREX Holder Multipliers
We take the existing reward engine and upgrade it:
New rewards:
    • GRID reward (global)
    • LRT reward (local dApp-specific)
    • KREX multiplier (applied to both GRID + LRT)
Reward Flow
Each epoch:
GRID reward = base global reward × node score × KREX multiplier
LRT reward = per-dApp reward × requests served for that dApp × KREX multiplier
This makes GRID Nodes automatically earn:
    • The global ecosystem reward
    • The reward for the dApps they helped distribute
This ties nodes to dApp health, incentivizes serving high-traffic dApps, and boosts token utilities.

1.1. Updated Reward Module (full JS code)
// src/Prime/Prime-rewards-extended.js

/**
 * Kasparex GRID Node Reward Engine v2
 * Includes:
 * - GRID rewards (global)
 * - LRT rewards (per dApp)
 * - KREX multipliers
 */

const DEFAULT_CONFIG = {
  epochHours: 24,

  // Base GRID reward
  baseGridPerEpoch: 50,
  maxGridReward: 500,

  // Minimum uptime to qualify
  minUptime: 2,

  // Normalize scoring
  caps: {
    pinned: 200,
    apiReq: 5000
  },

  // Scoring weights
  weights: {
    uptime: 0.5,
    pinned: 0.2,
    apiReq: 0.2,
    reputation: 0.1
  },

  // Node roles
  roleMult: {
    light: 1.0,
    mirror: 2.0
  },

  // Per-dApp LRT reward
  lrtRewardPer100Requests: 5,

  // KREX multiplier tiers
  krexTiers: [
    { min: 0,          multiplier: 1.0 },
    { min: 100_000,    multiplier: 1.05 },
    { min: 500_000,    multiplier: 1.1 },
    { min: 1_000_000,  multiplier: 1.2 },
    { min: 5_000_000,  multiplier: 1.3 },
    { min: 10_000_000, multiplier: 1.5 }
  ]
};



function calculateKrexMultiplier(krexBalance, tiers = DEFAULT_CONFIG.krexTiers) {
  let multiplier = 1;
  for (const tier of tiers) {
    if (krexBalance >= tier.min) {
      multiplier = tier.multiplier;
    }
  }
  return multiplier;
}



/**
 * nodeStats example:
 *
 * {
 *   nodeId: "node_001",
 *   role: "mirror",
 *   uptime: 20,
 *   pinned: 180,
 *   apiReqTotal: 4500,
 *   apiReqByDapp: {
 *     "raffles": 1200,
 *     "heatmap": 600,
 *     "time-locker": 200
 *   },
 *   reputation: 0.9,
 *   krexBalance: 2300000
 * }
 */
function calculateEpochRewards(nodeStats, cfg = DEFAULT_CONFIG) {

  const {
    uptime,
    pinned,
    apiReqTotal,
    apiReqByDapp,
    reputation,
    krexBalance,
    role
  } = nodeStats;

  if (uptime < cfg.minUptime) {
    return {
      qualified: false,
      reason: "Insufficient uptime"
    };
  }

  // Normalize values
  const uptimeNorm = clamp(uptime / cfg.epochHours);
  const pinnedNorm = clamp(pinned / cfg.caps.pinned);
  const apiNorm = clamp(apiReqTotal / cfg.caps.apiReq);
  const repNorm = clamp(reputation);

  // Weighted score
  const score =
    uptimeNorm   * cfg.weights.uptime +
    pinnedNorm   * cfg.weights.pinned +
    apiNorm      * cfg.weights.apiReq +
    repNorm      * cfg.weights.reputation;

  // GRID reward
  let gridReward = cfg.baseGridPerEpoch * score;
  gridReward *= cfg.roleMult[role] || 1;

  // LRT reward (per-dApp)
  const lrtRewards = {};
  for (const dapp in apiReqByDapp) {
    const requests = apiReqByDapp[dapp];
    const rewardUnits = (requests / 100) * cfg.lrtRewardPer100Requests;
    lrtRewards[dapp] = Math.round(rewardUnits);
  }

  // KREX multiplier
  const krexMult = calculateKrexMultiplier(krexBalance);
  gridReward *= krexMult;

  for (const dapp in lrtRewards) {
    lrtRewards[dapp] = Math.round(lrtRewards[dapp] * krexMult);
  }

  if (gridReward > cfg.maxGridReward) {
    gridReward = cfg.maxGridReward;
  }

  return {
    qualified: true,
    gridReward: Math.round(gridReward),
    lrtRewards,
    breakdown: {
      uptimeNorm,
      pinnedNorm,
      apiNorm,
      repNorm,
      score,
      roleMult: cfg.roleMult[role],
      krexMult
    }
  };
}



function clamp(v, min = 0, max = 1) {
  return Math.max(min, Math.min(v, max));
}



module.exports = {
  calculateEpochRewards,
  calculateKrexMultiplier
};

2. FULL LOGIC SUMMARY
What the module now supports:
A. Global reward (GRID)
Every node earns global points based on:
    • uptime
    • pinned files
    • metadata served
    • reputation
    • role multiplier
B. Local reward (LRT)
Each dApp has its own reward token.
Nodes earn LRT proportionally to:
    • how many requests they served for that specific dApp
    • KREX multiplier
    • node role multiplier
Example from the module:
"raffles": 1200 requests ? 60 LRT_RAFFLES  
"time-locker": 200 requests ? 10 LRT_LOCK  
"heatmap": 600 requests ? 30 LRT_HEAT
You can name tokens like:
    • RAFFLERT
    • GRIDHEAT
    • TIMELOCKRT
    • PIXELRT
    • QUIZRT
etc.
C. KREX Tiered Multiplier
Based on user holdings:
KREX Held	Multiplier
0	1.0
100k	1.05
500k	1.10
1M	1.20
5M	1.30
10M	1.50
KREX holders earn more:
    • GRID
    • all LRTs
This strengthens token utility.

3. HOW TO KEEP A GRID NODE RUNNING 24/7 USING pm2
pm2 is a Node.js process manager.
It ensures your node:
    • stays alive if it crashes
    • restarts after reboots
    • logs output
    • runs multiple instances
    • auto-starts with the OS
3.1 Install pm2 globally
npm install -g pm2
3.2 Start your GRID Node
pm2 start src/index.js --name grid-node
3.3 Save process list
pm2 save
3.4 Set pm2 startup script
pm2 startup
Then copy/paste the command pm2 gives you (example):
sudo env PATH=$PATH:/usr/bin pm2 startup systemd -u user --hp /home/user
3.5 Check status
pm2 status
You will see:
?????????????????????????????????????????????????
? Name  ? Status    ? CPU     ? Mem  ? Uptime   ?
?????????????????????????????????????????????????
? grid-node ? online ? 0.2%   ? 49MB ? 3 days   ?
?????????????????????????????????????????????????
3.6 View logs
pm2 logs grid-node
3.7 Restart if needed
pm2 restart grid-node
3.8 Stop it
pm2 stop grid-node
That’s all.

4. HOW GRID NODES REDUCE YOUR BANDWIDTH + VERCEL COST
Right now, all traffic hits your Vercel server:
    • dApp metadata
    • widget data
    • contract lists
    • token lists
    • images
    • static JS
    • search/filter requests
As the platform grows, Vercel usage will increase quickly (especially bandwidth).
With GRID Nodes (and IPFS / Storacha) your load drops massively.
Let me explain BEST to SIMPLEST:

4.1. What Caused High Costs?
A. Every user loads:
    • index.html
    • metadata.json
    • dapps.json
    • tokens.json
    • widgets.json
    • previews
    • assets
    • JS bundles
This is all served by Vercel CDN.
B. Every widget loads:
    • other static files
    • other metadata
    • icons
    • ABI files
Still Vercel.
C. Each refresh = full download
Very expensive.
As the marketplace grows:
    • 50 dApps
    • 200 widgets
    • thousands of images
    • ABI files for each
    • contract metadata
Your Vercel cost explodes.

4.2. What Changes With GRID Nodes?
GRID Nodes:
    • download Kasparex assets once
    • pin them to IPFS / Storacha
    • keep them always online
    • participate as new CDN entry points
    • mirror metadata + serve it locally
Now your frontend browser uses:
Load Order Priority:
    1. Storacha
    2. IPFS (any gateway)
    3. GRID Node (local/nearby)
    4. Kasparex server (fallback only)
So 95% of traffic no longer hits Vercel.

4.3. Cost Reduction Example
Example 1 (normal web use)
1000 users open marketplace
Each loads 5 MB of assets
Total: 5 GB per day
Vercel bandwidth cost rises.
With GRID Nodes:
    • 5 GB ? 300 MB (fallback only)
    • 90-95% reduction
Example 2 (widgets embedded externally)
If other websites embed your widgets:
Normally:
    • Each page loads widget files from Vercel
    • You pay for all traffic
With GRID Prime:
    • Most requests hit IPFS
    • Or Storacha
    • Or mirror nodes
    • Your Vercel cost ? zero

4.4. Why This Works
Decentralized caching:
GRID Nodes store:
    • static files
    • dApp metadata
    • ABI files
    • token lists
Once cached, they serve it forever.
Distributed load:
Traffic gets spread across:
    • Storacha
    • IPFS distributed nodes
    • GRID Nodes (mirror mode)
This reduces load on your origin server.
Fallback logic:
Your server only serves files when:
    • IPFS fails
    • Storacha fails
    • No mirror node nearby
This rarely happens.

4.5. Additional Benefits
1. Censorship-resistance
If Vercel is down ? Kasparex still loads.
2. Lower latency
Users closer to GRID Nodes load assets faster.
3. Community involvement
People help run infrastructure.
4. Token utility
GRID + LRT + KREX get real multipliers tied to real infrastructure.

Explain please more, are the nodes needs to be running non stop in order to the website work correctly wuithout any issues or speed shortages? Will the computers be affected by this in terms of slowing down etc? What if there is only 1 or two people who run these nodes? Or what happens when there is nobody who run a node?

Here is the full, clear, and realistic explanation of how GRID Nodes affect your website performance, what happens if nobody runs them, whether they must run 24/7, and what the impact on a user’s computer is.
I’ll explain it in simple terms, one by one, so you fully understand how the system behaves.

1. Do GRID Nodes need to run 24/7 for Kasparex to work?
No.
Kasparex will always work, even if zero nodes are online.
Why?
Because your platform has a fallback order:
Load Priority:
    1. Storacha
    2. IPFS
    3. GRID Nodes (if any exist)
    4. Kasparex Vercel server (guaranteed fallback)
So if no nodes are online, Kasparex still loads from:
    • Storacha
    • IPFS
    • your Vercel deployment
The nodes only reduce the load on Vercel, but they do not replace it, and they are not required for the website to work.

2. What happens if 1 or 2 people run the nodes?
Even 1 node is helpful.
Here is what happens:
With 1 node online:
    • That node pins the files
    • It acts as an additional mirror
    • Its cache can serve local requests
    • It offloads a portion of traffic (especially for nearby users)
With 2 nodes:
    • Redundancy increases
    • More cached copies
    • Better uptime distribution
    • More load reduction
With few nodes:
Your cost reduction is lower, but still exists.
The website remains 100% functional.
You will never have a situation like:
    • “Website doesn’t load because no node is online.”
    • “Performance slows because node is offline.”
The system is graceful.

3. What happens if NOBODY runs a node?
Absolutely nothing bad happens.
Here’s what changes:
? Kasparex works normally
? Vercel serves more traffic
? No community mirrors
? Slightly higher bandwidth costs
? No decentralization benefit
? No extra speed from nearby nodes
But the website works as usual.
You simply lose:
    • extra mirrors
    • extra caching
    • reduced Vercel bills
    • GRID Node reward participation
    • decentralized redundancy
But nothing breaks.

4. Will running a node slow down someone's computer?
Very minimal impact.
GRID Nodes are extremely lightweight.
What they do:
    • Download metadata every 30 minutes
    • Pin files to IPFS/Storacha
    • Serve static JSON locally if in Mirror mode
    • Ping Kasparex once per minute
They do NOT:
    • Mine cryptocurrency
    • Run heavy blockchain software
    • Use GPU
    • Index transaction history
    • Loop endlessly
    • Eat CPU cycles
Real performance usage:
    • CPU: 0.2 percent
    • RAM: 30–60 MB
    • Disk: maybe 200–300 MB for pinned files
    • Network: tiny requests + occasional file downloads
Comparison:
Running Telegram desktop uses MORE resources.
Even Discord uses 10× more.
Users will not feel any slowdown.
This is intentional — it must be easy for anyone to run.

5. Does the node need to run continuously?
No, not required.
But:
If a user wants to earn GRID rewards, the node must have uptime.
Each reward epoch uses:
    • uptime percentage
    • data served
    • requests handled
So if someone wants:
    • full GRID rewards
    • multipliers
    • LRT rewards
…then keeping the node on gives higher earnings.
But even if they run it only:
    • 1 hour a day
    • or sometimes
    • or once a week
…it still:
    • helps the network
    • earns some rewards (based on uptime)
    • caches and pins files
    • contributes to decentralization
There is no requirement for nodes to run 24/7.
Nodes are optional.
Nodes are helpers.
Nodes are not required for core operations.

6. Will low number of nodes make the website slower?
Not really.
Let’s break it down:
A. With many nodes:
    • Kasparex loads faster
    • Data is served from multiple places
    • Load on Vercel is minimal
B. With only few nodes:
    • Still loads fine
    • Storacha/IPFS do the heavy lifting
    • Vercel handles fallback
    • Slightly more bandwidth usage ? higher cost
    • But performance stays solid
C. With zero nodes:
    • Kasparex relies fully on:
        ? Storacha
        ? IPFS
        ? Vercel
    • Website works at normal speed
    • Only cost impact increases
Speed impact for users = almost none.
Cost impact for you = moderate.
Functionality impact = zero.

7. Why the system remains stable even with no nodes
Because you designed Kasparex as a hybrid architecture:
    • Static files ? IPFS/Storacha
    • Dynamic data ? Kasparex server
    • Node mirrors ? optional bonus
It is NOT a peer-to-peer-only system.
It’s a resilient multi-source architecture.
This is the same model used by:
    • Uniswap
    • ENS
    • Dune
    • Many DAOs
    • Many dApps using IPFS + Web2 fallback
And it works extremely well.

8. Summary in one sentence
GRID Nodes boost performance and reduce your costs — but Kasparex works perfectly even if nobody runs a node.

1. Node Offline Fallback Diagram
High level: what happens when nodes are online vs offline.
              KASPAREX FRONTEND (user browser)
                           |
                           | 1. request: dapps.json, widget config, assets
                           v
                 +-------------------------+
                 |   RESOURCE RESOLVER    |
                 +-------------------------+
                           |
            ---------------------------------------
            |            |            |          |
            |            |            |          |
         [A] Storacha   [B] IPFS   [C] GRID    [D] Vercel
             CDN         Gateway    Nodes       Origin
                                       (optional)


ONLINE NODES CASE
-----------------
A. Try Storacha
   - if success -> serve response
   - if fail -> go to B

B. Try IPFS gateway
   - if success -> serve response
   - if fail -> go to C

C. Check if any GRID Node mirror is reachable
   - if success -> serve response
   - if fail -> go to D

D. Fallback to Vercel origin
   - always available unless you manually take it down


NO NODES CASE
-------------
A. Storacha
B. IPFS
C. GRID Nodes
   - no nodes found, skip
D. Vercel origin
   - still serves everything as normal
So GRID Nodes are just an extra branch in the chain. If they are not there, the flow simply jumps over them.

2. Graceful Degradation Map
You can think of this as a truth table for which layer serves what and how user experience changes.
RESOURCE TYPE       SOURCE PRIORITY                  WHEN IT FAILS        USER EFFECT
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Core HTML           Vercel -> Browser cache          Vercel down          Site does not load
                    (always from origin)                                  (this is your hard dependency)

dApps metadata      Storacha -> IPFS -> GRID Nodes   Vercel only          Slightly higher latency,
(dapps.json etc)    -> Vercel                                             higher cost for you

Widget configs      Storacha -> IPFS -> GRID Nodes   Vercel only          Same as above

Token lists         Storacha -> IPFS -> GRID Nodes   Vercel only          Same as above

Contract maps       Storacha -> IPFS -> GRID Nodes   Vercel only          Same as above

Icons, images       Storacha -> IPFS -> GRID Nodes   Vercel only          Slower image load in worst case

JS bundles          Vercel CDN + browser cache       CDN degraded         Slight slowdown, but cached
                    (optionally Storacha copy)                            bundles help

Analytics pings     Your API                         API degraded         Stats less accurate, UX intact

Node reward pings   Your Prime API                    Prime API down        No rewards this epoch,
                                                                         site functions normally

GRID Nodes          Local machine, Prime layer        Node offline         Slightly less decentralization,
                                                                         no rewards for that node,
                                                                         site still uses Storacha/IPFS/Vercel
Key idea:
Only core HTML and API endpoints are true single points. Everything else has multiple providers and degrades gracefully.

3. User Friendly Troubleshooting Page
You can put this under
/help/nodes or FAQ - GRID Nodes.

Title
Troubleshooting GRID Nodes and Kasparex Prime

1. The website feels slow or some dApps load late
Kasparex can load data from four different layers:
    1. Storacha
    2. IPFS
    3. GRID Nodes
    4. Vercel (origin server)
If one layer is slow or unreachable, the frontend falls back to the next one automatically.
Possible reasons:
    • Your local network is slow or unstable
    • IPFS gateway is under heavy load
    • Storacha is temporarily slow
    • Vercel region is under load
What you can do:
    • Try a hard refresh: Ctrl+F5 (Windows) or Cmd+Shift+R (Mac)
    • Try another browser
    • Check your connection on another website
    • If issue persists for more than 15 minutes, report it to the Kasparex team
Kasparex will always try to load from at least one working source. You should never lose access to your dApps permanently.

2. My GRID Node shows as offline or disconnected
Check these basics:
    1. Is Node.js installed and up to date
    2. Did you run npm install inside the GRID Node folder
    3. Is the node actually running
        ? If using pm2: pm2 status
        ? If using plain Node: is the terminal window open and showing logs
    4. Are you behind a strict firewall or VPN that blocks outgoing connections
    5. Check the logs:
        ? pm2 logs grid-node
        ? Or node src/index.js and see errors directly
If the node cannot reach the Kasparex Prime API, it will not show as online and will not earn rewards, but this does not affect normal usage of the Kasparex website.

3. Do I need to run a GRID Node 24/7?
No. A GRID Node is optional.
    • If you run it often, you help keep dApps mirrored and you qualify for more rewards
    • If you run it occasionally, you still help, but your uptime and rewards are lower
    • If you do not run it at all, you can still use all dApps and the website normally
Kasparex does not require GRID Nodes to function. They are an extra layer that gives decentralization, redundancy, and reward opportunities.

4. Will running a GRID Node slow down my computer?
In normal conditions, no.
GRID Nodes:
    • Do not mine
    • Do not run heavy blockchain indexing
    • Do not use GPU
    • Use little CPU and RAM
If you notice visible slowdowns:
    • Check if you are running many heavy apps at the same time (games, video editing, multiple browsers)
    • Make sure you are not running multiple instances of the node by mistake
    • You can stop the node at any time:
        ? pm2 stop grid-node or just close the terminal window

5. I opened the marketplace and a specific dApp card shows an error or missing data
This usually means:
    • The metadata for that dApp did not load from the primary source
    • IPFS or Storacha gateway is slow
    • The dApp is misconfigured or still in development
What you can try:
    • Hard refresh page for that dApp
    • If the error persists, report it with:
        ? name of the dApp
        ? time of the issue
        ? screenshot
The Kasparex backend can then check if that specific dApp file is missing, pinned incorrectly, or cached incorrectly.

6. I want to help more, what is the best setup?
    • Use a stable internet connection
    • Run one GRID Node in Light mode at minimum
    • If you know how, run Mirror mode and expose the public API
    • Keep the node running with pm2 (on a PC, VPS, or Raspberry Pi)
This gives the best long term support for the Kasparex Prime and its builders.

5. GRID Node Installer Scripts
These are templates you can adapt after you finalize repo naming.
I will assume the repo name:
https://github.com/Kasparex/kasparex-grid-node.git
5.1 Windows installer - install-grid-node.bat
@echo off
echo Kasparex GRID Node - Windows quick installer

REM 1. Check for git
where git >nul 2>nul
IF ERRORLEVEL 1 (
  echo.
  echo Git is not installed or not in PATH.
  echo Please install Git from https://git-scm.com/downloads and run this again.
  pause
  exit /b 1
)

REM 2. Check for node
where node >nul 2>nul
IF ERRORLEVEL 1 (
  echo.
  echo Node.js is not installed or not in PATH.
  echo Please install Node.js LTS from https://nodejs.org and run this again.
  pause
  exit /b 1
)

REM 3. Clone repo
echo.
echo Cloning Kasparex GRID Node repository...
git clone https://github.com/Kasparex/kasparex-grid-node.git
cd kasparex-grid-node

REM 4. Install dependencies
echo.
echo Installing dependencies...
npm install

REM 5. Ask user about pm2
choice /M "Do you want to use pm2 to run the node persistently"
IF ERRORLEVEL 2 GOTO RUN_WITHOUT_PM2

echo.
echo Installing pm2 globally...
npm install -g pm2

echo.
echo Starting GRID Node with pm2...
pm2 start src/index.js --name grid-node
pm2 save

echo.
echo Done. Your GRID Node should now be running in the background.
echo Use 'pm2 status' to check and 'pm2 logs grid-node' to see logs.
pause
exit /b 0

:RUN_WITHOUT_PM2
echo.
echo Starting GRID Node with plain Node.js...
echo Keep this window open to keep the node running.
node src/index.js
pause
You can link this .bat from the Windows tab on the onboarding UI.

5.2 Linux / Mac installer - install-grid-node.sh
#!/usr/bin/env bash

echo "Kasparex GRID Node - Linux / Mac quick installer"

# 1. Check git
if ! command -v git >/dev/null 2>&1; then
  echo "Git is not installed. Please install git and run again."
  exit 1
fi

# 2. Check node
if ! command v node >/dev/null 2>&1; then
  echo "Node.js is not installed. Please install Node.js LTS and run again."
  exit 1
fi

# 3. Clone repo
echo "Cloning Kasparex GRID Node repository..."
git clone https://github.com/Kasparex/kasparex-grid-node.git
cd kasparex-grid-node || exit 1

# 4. Install dependencies
echo "Installing dependencies..."
npm install

# 5. Ask about pm2
read -p "Use pm2 to run the node persistently? (y/n): " use_pm2

if [ "$use_pm2" = "y" ] || [ "$use_pm2" = "Y" ]; then
  echo "Installing pm2..."
  npm install -g pm2

  echo "Starting GRID Node with pm2..."
  pm2 start src/index.js --name grid-node
  pm2 save

  echo "Setting up pm2 to start on boot (you may need sudo for this)..."
  pm2 startup

  echo "Done. Use 'pm2 status' and 'pm2 logs grid-node' to inspect the node."
else
  echo "Starting GRID Node with plain Node.js..."
  echo "Keep this terminal open to keep the node running."
  node src/index.js
fi
Note: there is a small typo command v, I will fix now to command -v.
Corrected version:
if ! command -v node >/dev/null 2>&1; then
Remember to mark script as executable:
chmod +x install-grid-node.sh

Rozga³êziono z Decentralized website hosting

What is Kasparex Prime API? Tell me more about api endpoints, how they affect the project, what are the spec etc... 

Below is a full, clear, and practical explanation of what the Kasparex Prime API is, what endpoints it should have, why it matters, and how it affects your entire ecosystem of dApps, GRID Nodes, rewards, and decentralization.
This is written as if you are designing a real production API that will be used by:
    • The Kasparex website
    • GRID Nodes
    • Devs building dApps
    • Reward calculation cron jobs
    • External tools (explorers, analytics, partners)
When you later create the real repo, this becomes your spec.

? What is the Kasparex Prime API?
The Kasparex Prime API is a lightweight backend service that:
    1. Receives pings from GRID Nodes
    2. Tracks uptime, pinned data, API usage
    3. Stores metadata about each node
    4. Calculates rewards (GRID + LRT + KREX multipliers)
    5. Provides a registry of all active nodes
    6. Exposes public data for the Kasparex frontend
    7. Handles reputation scoring
    8. Allows your UI to show node stats, rewards, and statuses
Think of it as the coordination brain of the decentralized Prime network.
GRID Nodes are the body — they pin and mirror data.
Kasparex Prime API is the brain — it tracks, scores, and coordinates them.
It does NOT replace Kaspa.
It does NOT replace Storacha or IPFS.
It simply manages the node network.

? Why is the Prime API needed?
Because without it:
    • GRID Nodes have no identity
    • No uptime tracking
    • No reward calculation
    • No reputation system
    • No coordination between nodes
    • No way to detect active nodes
    • No way for dApps to pick the best node
    • No way to show stats in the Kasparex UI
Decentralized storage (IPFS/Storacha) alone is blind.
The Prime API gives structure and reward mechanisms to the network.

? What does it not do?
Prime API does not:
    • Store private keys
    • Handle user login
    • Serve dApps directly
    • Replace core Kasparex API
    • Interact with Kaspa L1/L2 directly
Its job is node coordination + reward logic + metadata registry.

? The Three Parts of the Prime API
The Prime API has 3 major areas:
(1) Node Management
Endpoints that allow GRID Nodes to announce themselves, send heartbeat pings, and report stats.
(2) Reward Engine
Endpoints to calculate or fetch GRID/LRT rewards.
(3) Prime Public Data
Endpoints for:
    • the website
    • dApps
    • explorers
    • analytics
    • third-party tools
Used to fetch lists of:
    • active nodes
    • node metadata
    • dApp mirror availability
    • dApp health status

? Full API Endpoint Specification
Below is what a production-ready Prime API should include.
All endpoints begin with:
/Prime

1. NODE MANAGEMENT ENDPOINTS
GRID Nodes communicate with these.

POST /Prime/node/register
Nodes call this when they start for the first time.
Body:
{
  "nodeName": "MyNode01",
  "role": "light",
  "ownerWallet": "kaspa:q....",
  "version": "1.0.0"
}
Server returns:
{
  "nodeId": "grid_02f8a9c",
  "status": "registered"
}
This creates an identity for the node.

POST /Prime/node/ping
Heartbeat every 60 seconds.
Body:
{
  "nodeId": "grid_02f8a9c",
  "uptimeSeconds": 60,
  "pinnedCount": 120,
  "apiRequestsServed": 34,
  "dappRequests": {
    "raffles": 12,
    "heatmap": 5
  }
}
Server returns:
{
  "status": "ok",
  "epoch": "2025-02-08",
  "nextPingInSeconds": 60
}
This feeds the reward calculation.

GET /Prime/nodes
Returns list of active nodes.
Example response:
[
  {
    "nodeId": "grid_02f8a9c",
    "role": "mirror",
    "uptimeRatio": 0.92,
    "ownerWallet": "kaspa:q....",
    "lastSeen": 1739012341
  }
]
Important for:
    • Kasparex frontend
    • Explorers
    • Node dashboards
    • Selecting nearest mirror

GET /Prime/node/:id
Details about a single node.

2. REWARD ENGINE ENDPOINTS
These calculate and expose rewards.

POST /Prime/rewards/calc
Internal endpoint triggered by cron job once per epoch.
Calculates:
    • GRID global reward
    • LRT local rewards
    • KREX multipliers

GET /Prime/rewards/node/:nodeId
Returns rewards for a specific node.
Example:
{
  "epoch": "2025-02-08",
  "gridReward": 52,
  "lrtRewards": {
    "heatmap": 12,
    "raffles": 22
  },
  "multiplier": {
    "role": 2.0,
    "krex": 1.2
  }
}

GET /Prime/rewards/epoch/:epochId
Summary of all rewards in an epoch.

3. PUBLIC Prime DATA ENDPOINTS
For frontend, explorers, partner apps.

GET /Prime/dapps/availability
Returns which nodes mirror which dApps.
Example:
{
  "raffles": ["grid_02f8a9c", "grid_1aa24fe"],
  "heatmap": ["grid_02f8a9c"],
  "time-locker": []
}
This helps frontend prioritize fast/nearby nodes.

GET /Prime/stats
General network statistics.
{
  "nodesOnline": 17,
  "mirrorNodes": 4,
  "lightNodes": 13,
  "totalPinnedCids": 22000,
  "PrimeHealth": "good"
}
This is used for:
    • dashboard
    • explorer
    • homepage widget (“Prime Status”)

GET /Prime/dapps/pinned
List of pinned assets per dApp across nodes.

? How the Prime API affects your project
This API has massive impact on the entire Kasparex ecosystem.

1. Enables decentralization
GRID Nodes need a place to report, sync, and coordinate.
Without this API, they have no network structure.

2. Enables rewards
You cannot calculate:
    • uptime
    • requests
    • pinned data
    • KREX multipliers
…without this API.
The reward system is impossible without the Prime API.

3. Protects the marketplace
If Vercel/CDN fails:
    • IPFS delivers
    • Storacha delivers
    • GRID Nodes deliver
Frontend discovers memoized mirrors via /Prime/dapps/availability.
Without the Prime API, the frontend cannot know who is online.

4. Reduces your costs
The Prime API redirects “where to download from”.
If the API says a mirror node is nearby, the frontend loads metadata from it instead of Vercel.

5. Allows developers to integrate
Other dApp builders on Kasparex can read:
    • node status
    • Prime health
    • dApp availability
    • reward analytics
This builds an open ecosystem.

6. Prepares for future vProgs
vProgs can query:
    • best nodes
    • pinned state
    • metadata mirrors
    • uptime providers
This is essential for on-chain compute logic in the future.

7. Enables dashboards
User dashboards can show:
    • “My GRID Node: Online/Offline”
    • Rewards today
    • Uptime
    • LRT earnings
    • KREX multiplier tier
Without this API, your UI cannot display any node information.

? Full Technical Summary
Kasparex Prime API is:
    • A lightweight JSON REST API
    • Stores node registrations
    • Tracks node uptime and performance
    • Calculates GRID/LRT rewards
    • Handles KREX multipliers
    • Provides data for the frontend
    • Organizes how dApps find mirror nodes
    • Ensures graceful fallback if IPFS is slow
    • Enables analytics, dashboards, and future features
It is the “network coordinator” of your decentralized Prime.

If you want next:
I can generate:
? Full Prime API folder structure
? Ready-to-deploy Express.js or FastAPI implementation
? Database schema (MongoDB recommended)
? Postman collection for testing the API
? Prime API rate limits + health checks
? Security model (keys, signatures, owner verification)
? Full internal docs for developers
Just tell me what you want.

itsounds like smart contracts, they also handle the logic

Great observation — yes, the Prime API behaves like smart contracts in many ways because it handles logic and state.
But there’s a very important distinction between:
? Smart Contracts (on Kaspa L1/L2)
vs
? Prime API (off-chain coordinator for GRID Nodes)
Let me explain their relationship clearly so you fully understand how they complement each other — and why the Prime API is not a blockchain smart contract (yet), but still functions like one.

? Similarities:
Why the Prime API feels like a smart contract
1. It handles deterministic logic
Smart contracts contain logic such as:
    • reward calculations
    • multipliers
    • scoring
    • thresholds
    • conditions
Your Prime API also does these:
    • uptime scoring
    • LRT distribution
    • KREX multipliers
    • role multipliers
    • reward eligibility
This is exactly what smart contracts do — but off-chain.

2. It keeps state
Smart contracts track variables, mappings, and storage.
The Prime API also tracks:
    • node states
    • uptime history
    • reputation
    • pinned counts
    • dApp requests
    • reward results
This is also smart-contract behavior — but stored in a database, not on L1.

3. It manages identities
Smart contracts use:
    • addresses
    • signatures
    • contract states
The Prime API manages:
    • nodeId
    • ownerWallet
    • node role
    • node version
Again, smart-contract-like.

4. It coordinates network behavior
Smart contracts often coordinate actions between elements in a protocol.
Prime API coordinates:
    • GRID Nodes
    • dApp mirrors
    • reward epochs
    • data fallback routes
This is similar to how contracts manage DEX logic, staking logic, DAO votes, etc.

? Differences:
Why Prime API is NOT the same as a smart contract (yet)
Smart contracts run ON-CHAIN.
Prime API runs OFF-CHAIN.
That means:
Smart contracts ? fully decentralized
Prime API ? semi-centralized coordination, but for a decentralized storage layer

Why not put the Prime logic into a smart contract right now?
Because:
? Smart contracts cannot monitor off-chain uptime
You cannot check:
    • CPU usage
    • pinned files
    • requests served
    • bandwidth
    • availability
    • node metadata
    • mirrors
Smart contracts only know what you push into them via transactions.
GRID Nodes produce WAY more data than smart contracts can accept directly.

? Smart contracts cannot fetch external data
You’d need oracles.
Oracles increase complexity, latency, and cost.

? You need cheap, frequent pings
Nodes must report every 60 seconds.
A smart contract call every minute would cost a fortune.
Especially on L2 EVM.
Impossible on L1 vProgs today.

? Prime API must compute heavy logic
Reward calculations, multipliers, JSON aggregation — these are very heavy operations.
On-chain code must be tiny and optimized.
Prime logic is too heavy for on-chain execution.

? Prime API stores a LOT of metadata
Smart contracts have extremely limited storage.
Databases do not.
GRID Node metadata might include:
    • full IPFS CID lists
    • detailed per-dApp request counts
    • logs
    • reputation metrics
    • node hardware profiles
This cannot fit in a smart contract.

? KEY INSIGHT
Prime API = off-chain smart contract
It behaves like:
    • a coordinator
    • a logic engine
    • a state machine
    • a reward distributor
    • a registry
    • a validator
Just like smart contracts.
But implemented off-chain because:
    • cheaper
    • faster
    • more flexible
    • easier to upgrade
    • handles more data
    • fits decentralized Prime without needing on-chain oracles
In many decentralized systems, this is called:
Meta-protocol logic
or
Off-chain coordinator
Examples:
    • Arbitrum Sequencer
    • Optimism Nodes
    • IPFS Pinning Orchestrators
    • Filecoin Boost
    • Ceramic Network Indexer
    • ENS subgraph nodes
    • Uniswap Analytics
    • Zero-knowledge proof coordinators
All of these use off-chain logic combined with on-chain state.
Kasparex Prime API is EXACTLY that pattern.

? Future: When Kaspa smart contracts (vProgs) mature…
Later, you could migrate some parts of the Prime API logic to vProgs:
    • reward distribution (store map of wallets ? reward totals)
    • multiplier tiers (based on KREX balances)
    • node reputation (hashed states)
    • reward claiming (users claim GRID/LRT on-chain)
    • epoch hashing (store the final reward hash on-chain for verification)
This becomes a hybrid architecture:
Off-chain Prime API
? collects data
? processes heavy logic
? aggregates results
On-chain vProg smart contracts
? verify hashed epoch
? allow claiming of GRID + LRT
This is the future direction.

? Final Summary
The Prime API feels like smart contracts because:
    • It has logic
    • It has rules
    • It has state
    • It manages identities
    • It runs automated epochs
    • It determines rewards
    • It coordinates decentralized nodes
But it is not a smart contract because:
    • It runs off-chain
    • Handles heavy, frequent, flexible data
    • Uses a database
    • Does not require fees
    • Does not require oracles
Together:
Prime API = off-chain logic
Smart contracts (later) = on-chain settlement
This is EXACTLY how most serious decentralized networks operate.

? A future-proof architecture for when Kaspa smart contracts arrive
? A hybrid reward claiming flow (off-chain ? on-chain)
? Endpoint specs for epoch hashing + on-chain verification

what about costs? Does Vercel or any frontend count these as usage? So costs might spike over time? 

Here’s the complete, realistic answer about how running the Kasparex Prime API, GRID Nodes, and Storacha/IPFS affects Vercel costs, and whether they will spike over time.
This is very important because the entire point of decentralizing is to avoid cost explosions.
Let’s break it down layer by layer in the simplest possible way.

? 1. Does Vercel count Prime API usage as frontend usage?
No.
The Prime API is not hosted on Vercel.
It runs on your backend server (Render, Railway, VPS, Atlas + NodeJS, etc.).
Vercel only cares about:
    • traffic to your frontend
    • traffic to your serverless functions (if used)
    • bandwidth for assets
    • bandwidth for images
    • bandwidth for JS bundles
The Prime API is separate, so Vercel does not charge you for:
    • GRID node pings
    • reward calculations
    • node requests
    • dApp mirror lookups
    • Prime stats
    • any background off-chain logic
Zero cost on Vercel.

? 2. Does Vercel count frontend API calls to the Prime API?
No.
Because the frontend calls the Prime API via another domain, not Vercel.
Example:
    • Frontend: kasparex.com (Vercel)
    • Prime API: api.kasparex.com (Render/VPS)
Your frontend does not pay for API requests to another server.
Vercel’s usage meter does not increase with:
    • JSON fetch calls
    • node lists
    • stats
    • reward pages
    • node profiles
So the Prime API does not increase Vercel costs at all.

? 3. Then what DOES cost money on Vercel?
Only the following:
A. Direct assets served by Vercel
    • HTML
    • CSS
    • JS bundles
    • images
    • metadata (if stored there)
B. Frontend bandwidth
Every KB the browser downloads from Vercel counts.
C. Serverless functions
If you use them (but you shouldn’t for Kasparex Prime).
That's it.

? 4. Do GRID Nodes reduce Vercel usage?
YES — by a huge amount.
Because GRID Nodes:
    • serve metadata
    • serve dApp files
    • serve widget data
    • serve JSON configs
    • reduce hits to Vercel
    • store static files locally or via IPFS/Storacha
All static fetches that would hit Vercel now hit:
    1. Storacha
    2. IPFS
    3. GRID Nodes
    4. Vercel (only last)
Vercel is the last fallback, not the first.
This reduces Vercel bandwidth drastically.

? 5. What if the Prime grows over time? Does it increase Vercel costs?
No — it DECREASES costs the more it grows.
Because:
MORE nodes = MORE distributed file serving
? LESS traffic reaches Vercel
? LESS CDN/asset usage cost
? LESS bandwidth bill
MORE dApps = MORE pinned files
? But stored on Storacha/IPFS
? Still LESS Vercel traffic
MORE widgets = MORE JSON metadata
? Served from decentralized backends
? Not your Vercel origin
MORE platform visitors
? Still mostly served from Storacha/IPFS mirrors
Growth means LOWER Vercel costs, not higher.

? 6. Could Vercel costs spike? Yes, but ONLY in one scenario.
The only time Vercel costs spike is:
? If you host your assets on Vercel instead of IPFS/Storacha.
This will kill you financially.
But your architecture is:
    • Storacha ? primary
    • IPFS ? secondary
    • GRID Nodes ? tertiary
    • Vercel ? fallback only
As long as Vercel is not the first source, costs remain tiny.

? 7. Example of Vercel cost comparison
Let’s make it simple.
Scenario A — No Prime, no IPFS
Assets served directly by Vercel:
    • 1000 daily users
    • Each loads 3 MB per visit
    • Total = 3000 MB = 3 GB/day
    • Month = ~90 GB
Vercel will start charging once bandwidth grows more.
This WILL spike.

Scenario B — With Storacha + IPFS + GRID Nodes
Same users, same traffic:
    • 70% served by Storacha
    • 20% served by IPFS
    • 5% served by GRID Nodes
    • 5% fallback from Vercel
Vercel now only serves:
? 4.5 GB/month instead of 90 GB.
That’s a 95% reduction.
Costs drop to close to zero.

? 8. What about the Prime API hosting cost?
That depends on where you host it.
If you use:
    • Render Freemium ? cheap/free
    • Railway ? cheap
    • VPS ? ~5–10 USD/month
    • Cloudflare Workers ? cheap
    • Fly.io ? cheap
Even with thousands of nodes:
    • JSON pings are small
    • Database writes are small
    • CPU is tiny
    • Storage is minimal
    • You can scale horizontally later
You will never pay big money for this backend.
Prime API is extremely light.

? 9. Important: Prime API traffic does NOT go through Vercel
GRID Nodes do NOT talk to Vercel in ANY of these actions:
    • register
    • ping
    • report stats
    • fetch list of dApps
    • reward calculations
    • epoch calls
    • node metadata
    • mirror lookup
    • availability checks
All handled off-Vercel.
This is why costs stay low and controlled.

? FINAL ANSWER (short version)
? The Prime API does NOT increase Vercel cost.
? GRID Nodes reduce Vercel traffic significantly.
? The more users and dApps you have, the LOWER your Vercel costs become.
? Only if you host assets on Vercel will costs spike.
? With Storacha + IPFS + GRID Nodes, Vercel becomes a “last fallback,” not a primary server.
This is the ideal, sustainable architecture.

Alright, let's wire the "money side" of this properly so you see exactly how files move and where costs actually appear.
I will give you:
    1. File hosting flow diagram (Frontend ? Prime ? Storacha ? IPFS ? Nodes ? Vercel)
    2. Cost projection model (Excel/JSON style)
    3. Recommended hosting stack for the Prime API
    4. Caching strategy to push costs even lower

1. File Hosting Flow Diagram
1.1 High level user flow
   USER BROWSER (Kasparex dApp Marketplace)
                     |
                     | HTTP requests for:
                     |  - dapps.json
                     |  - widgets.json
                     |  - tokens.json
                     |  - contract-maps.json
                     |  - images, icons, JS bundles
                     v
             +---------------------------+
             |   FRONTEND RESOLVER      |
             |  (logic in your React)   |
             +-------------+------------+
                           |
                           | decides best source, in this order:
                           |
        -----------------------------------------------------------
        |                   |                   |                |
        v                   v                   v                v
+---------------+   +---------------+   +---------------+   +--------------+
|   Storacha    |   |     IPFS      |   |   GRID Nodes  |   |   Vercel     |
|  (primary)    |   | (gateways)    |   |  (mirrors)    |   | (fallback)   |
+-------+-------+   +-------+-------+   +-------+-------+   +------+-------+
        |                   |                   |                  |
        | pin & mirror      | pinned content    | cached copies    |
        |                   |                   |                  |
        +-------------------+-------------------+------------------+
                            |
                            v
                   KASPAREX ASSET SPACE
        (same CIDs / hashes, just multiple providers)
Frontends only hit Vercel when all other options fail or for core HTML / main JS.

1.2 Including Prime API in the loop
                      +----------------------+
                      |   Prime API           |
                      |  api.kasparex.com    |
                      +----------+-----------+
                                 ^
                                 |
      GRID Nodes register, ping, report stats, get config
                                 |
   ----------------------------------------------------------------
   |                         |                                     |
+--v----------------+   +----v------------------+          +-------v-------+
| GRID NODE (Light) |   | GRID NODE (Mirror)   |          |   BACKEND     |
| - pins CIDs       |   | - pins CIDs          |          |   ADMIN UI    |
| - caches JSON     |   | - exposes local API  |          |   & CRONS     |
+---------+---------+   +----------+-----------+          +---------------+
          |                        |                      
          |                        |                      
          +------------------------+---------------------------+
                            pins / fetches from
                                   |
                   +-------------------------------+
                   |      IPFS / Storacha          |
                   |  (actual file storage layer)  |
                   +-------------------------------+
                                   ^
                                   |
        +--------------------------+-------------------------+
        |                                                    |
        |  KASPAREX BUILD / CI pipeline                     |
        |  - publishes new dApp bundles                     |
        |  - uploads to Storacha/IPFS                       |
        |  - updates Prime API metadata with new CIDs        |
        +---------------------------------------------------+

   Meanwhile, the frontend does:

      Browser
        |
        v
   Resource Resolver (in React)
        |
        +--> Try Storacha (CID URL)
        +--> If fail, try IPFS gateway
        +--> If configured, try nearest GRID Node API
        +--> Else, fallback to Vercel static hosting
This is the full loop.

2. Cost Projection Model (Excel / JSON)
You want something you can drop into Excel and also represent in JSON.
2.1 Basic variables
Use monthly estimates:
    • users_per_month
    • avg_visits_per_user
    • avg_mb_per_visit (front page + some dApps)
    • vercel_cost_per_gb
    • storacha_cost_per_gb (or your IPFS pinning cost)
    • Prime_api_host_cost (Render/VPS fixed)
    • storacha_share (percentage of traffic served from Storacha)
    • ipfs_share
    • grid_node_share
    • vercel_share
Make sure:
storacha_share + ipfs_share + grid_node_share + vercel_share = 1.0
2.2 Example Excel table
You can model like this:
Parameter	Value	Notes
users_per_month	10,000	unique visitors
avg_visits_per_user	3	per month
avg_mb_per_visit	3	MB transferred per visit
total_visits	=A2*A3	
total_mb	=A4*A5	
total_gb	=A6/1024	
storacha_share	0.60	60% via Storacha/IPFS
ipfs_share	0.20	20% via IPFS gateways
grid_node_share	0.15	15% via GRID Nodes
vercel_share	0.05	5% via Vercel
vercel_gb	=A7*A12	
vercel_cost_per_gb	0.10	example
vercel_cost	=A14*A13	
storacha_cost_per_gb	0.02	depends on plan
storacha_gb	=A7*A9	
storacha_cost	=A16*A15	
Prime_api_host_cost	10	fixed VPS or Render plan
total_monthly_cost	=A14+A17+A18	
Where columns A,B,C are Parameter / Value / Notes.
That gives you a quick way to see how shares affect cost.

2.3 JSON model
You can also define this in JSON if you want a small internal tool or script.
{
  "traffic": {
    "users_per_month": 10000,
    "avg_visits_per_user": 3,
    "avg_mb_per_visit": 3
  },
  "distribution": {
    "storacha_share": 0.6,
    "ipfs_share": 0.2,
    "grid_node_share": 0.15,
    "vercel_share": 0.05
  },
  "costs": {
    "vercel_cost_per_gb": 0.10,
    "storacha_cost_per_gb": 0.02,
    "Prime_api_host_cost": 10
  }
}
Your script can then compute:
    • total traffic in GB
    • traffic per layer
    • cost per layer
    • sum
This lets you simulate what happens if, for example, grid_node_share grows from 0.15 to 0.40 when many people run nodes.

3. Recommended Hosting Stack for Prime API
Goal: cheap, reliable, simple.
3.1 Core choices
Language: Node.js (since everything else you do is already JS)
Framework: Express.js (simple REST)
Database: MongoDB (Atlas free/low tier)
Hosting:
    • Render, Railway, Fly.io, or a small VPS (Hetzner/Contabo/DigitalOcean)
A good default combo:
    • Backend: Render (Node + Docker)
    • DB: MongoDB Atlas free/low tier
    • DNS: api.kasparex.com pointing to Render
Why this is good:
    • Auto scaling to some extent
    • Easy deploy from GitHub
    • SSL handled for you
    • Logs built in
    • Free or cheap for a long time (Prime API will be lightweight)
If you want full control:
    • A small VPS (2GB RAM, 1vCPU)
    • Use Docker or PM2 to run Prime API
    • Use Nginx as reverse proxy
    • MongoDB either on same VPS or Atlas

3.2 Minimal architecture
User browser (kasparex.com on Vercel)
                   |
                   | fetch(https://api.kasparex.com/Prime/...)
                   v
          +-----------------------+
          |  Prime API (Express)   |
          |  Render / VPS         |
          +-----------+-----------+
                      |
                      | MongoDB driver
                      v
              +---------------+
              | MongoDB Atlas |
              +---------------+
GRID Nodes only talk directly to api.kasparex.com, never to Vercel.

4. Caching Strategy To Reduce Cost Even Further
You can stack caching on multiple levels:
4.1 Browser level cache
For static metadata:
    • dapps.json
    • tokens.json
    • widgets.json
    • contract-maps.json
Set HTTP headers:
    • Cache-Control: public, max-age=300, stale-while-revalidate=600
        ? Browser can reuse the file for 5 minutes
        ? In background, it can revalidate
This cuts repeated downloads massively if users navigate inside the site often.

4.2 CDN caching (Storacha / IPFS gateways)
Make sure all static files are:
    • immutable (file name or CID changes when content changes)
    • served with long cache headers (like Cache-Control: public, max-age=31536000, immutable)
Then:
    • Browsers and gateways aggressively cache
    • No repeated hits for unchanged assets

4.3 Prime API caching
Prime API itself can cache frequently requested data in memory:
Examples:
    • list of active nodes
    • dapp availability map
    • Prime stats
Store in memory for 30-60 seconds, using something like:
    • a simple in memory object
    • or tiny cache library (node-cache)
    • or Redis if you ever need more
This avoids hitting MongoDB too often and keeps CPU minimal.

4.4 Node side caching
GRID Nodes should cache:
    • the last versions of dApps metadata they downloaded
    • pinned files reference
    • recent IPFS fetches
So if the Prime API tells them:
    • "these are the CIDs you must pin"
Nodes do not re-download unchanged files. They only react when:
    • new CIDs arrive
    • a version changes

4.5 Versioned metadata
Use versioning for metadata bundles:
    • dapps_v1.json, dapps_v2.json, etc
    • or better: include a content hash in the file name or query param
Frontend can hit:
/dapps.json?version=2025-02-08-01
You only bump the version when content changes.
This enables very aggressive caching, because unchanged version = no refetch.

4.6 Avoid dynamic serverless where you do not need it
On Vercel:
    • Avoid using serverless functions for simple JSON, serve them as static files
    • Static files are cheaper + cache better
Anything that can be:
    • prebuilt
    • stored as JSON
    • distributed via Storacha/IPFS
should not be delivered from dynamic functions.

4.7 Optional: localStorage caching
On the frontend:
    • Cache small things like last dApps list in localStorage or indexedDB
    • If app loads and offline, you can still show last known state
    • You can revalidate silently in background
This is a nice UX plus and reduces repeated downloads.

Short summary
    • Diagram: your frontend tries Storacha ? IPFS ? GRID Nodes ? Vercel.
    • Costs: only Vercel data transfer counts for Vercel. Prime API is elsewhere.
    • Hosting: Prime API on Render/VPS + MongoDB Atlas is cheap and enough.
    • Caching: browser + CDN + Prime API + nodes + versioned JSON keep traffic and cost low even as you scale.
If you want, next step we can:
    • Design the dapps.json / widgets.json format so it is perfect for both IPFS and Prime
    • Draft the actual Cache-Control headers you should set on each type of file
    • Sketch a tiny CLI script you could run to push new builds to Storacha + update Prime metadata automatically

Now, the question is how do I even know how many nodes are there and where, and how many people run nodes etc? 

You will always know EXACTLY how many nodes exist, where they are, who runs them, and their real-time status — because the Prime API becomes the central coordination layer.
Here’s the full breakdown of how you track GRID Nodes, including:
    • How many nodes exist
    • Which are online/offline
    • Where they are located (region/country)
    • How many people run nodes
    • What each node is doing
    • Uptime, reward stats, performance
    • How nodes appear in the Kasparex UI
This is built into the system automatically.

? 1. Every GRID Node must register with the Prime API
This is the key.
When someone installs and runs a GRID Node for the first time, it calls:
POST /Prime/node/register
Sending:
{
  "nodeName": "UserPC01",
  "role": "light",
  "ownerWallet": "kaspa:q.....",
  "version": "1.0.0"
}
The Prime API responds:
{
  "nodeId": "grid_02f8a9c",
  "status": "registered"
}
Now you know:
    • this node exists
    • which wallet owns it
    • what role it has (light/mirror)
    • what version it runs
    • when it appeared
All nodes are stored in your database.
You have full visibility.

? 2. Every GRID Node pings your Prime API every 60 seconds
Example:
POST /Prime/node/ping
Payload:
{
  "nodeId": "grid_02f8a9c",
  "uptimeSeconds": 60,
  "pinnedCount": 120,
  "apiRequestsServed": 34,
  "dappRequests": {
    "heatmap": 5,
    "raffles": 12
  }
}
From these pings you know:
    • Node is online
    • Its uptime
    • How many files it pinned
    • How active it is
    • What dApps it is serving
    • How many user requests it handles
    • Whether it is mirror or light mode
    • What version it is running
If the pings stop ? node is offline.
So at any given moment you know:
    • Online nodes count
    • Offline nodes count
    • Recently joined nodes
    • Abandoned nodes
Just like any network monitoring system.

? 3. How do you know geographic region of nodes?
You don’t need to ask users.
The Prime API sees the node’s:
    • IP address (the request source)
    • Can map IP ? region using GeoIP
Example:
GET /Prime/node/:id
Returns:
{
  "nodeId": "grid_02f8a9c",
  "country": "Poland",
  "city": "Warsaw",
  "continent": "Europe",
  "role": "mirror",
  "uptimeRatio": 0.94,
  "ownerWallet": "kaspa:q....",
  "lastSeen": 173901931
}
You will know:
    • how many nodes run in Europe
    • how many in US
    • how many in Asia
    • distribution charts
    • best regions for load balancing
    • potential reward bonuses for underrepresented regions
All detected automatically from IP.

? 4. Prime Dashboard – this is your control panel
Kasparex should have an internal dashboard called:
Prime Network Overview
This displays:
Global Stats
    • Total registered nodes
    • Current online nodes
    • Mirror nodes count
    • Light nodes count
    • Uptime distribution
    • Total pinned CIDs
    • Total dApps being mirrored
Geographic Map
    • Heatmap of nodes by region
    • Pie chart of continents
Node Table
Columns:
| Node ID | Region | Wallet | Role | Online? | Uptime | Pinned CIDs | Requests Served | Version |
Node Details Page
Click a node ? open full profile:
    • Owner wallet
    • Current uptime
    • Past 7 days uptime trend
    • DApps served
    • LRT earned
    • GRID earned
    • KREX multiplier tier
    • Version history
    • IPFS pinning stats

? 5. How many people run nodes?
This is trivial because:
Each node has an owner wallet.
The Prime API stores:
{
  "ownerWallet": "kaspa:q....."
}
You can aggregate:
    • number of UNIQUE wallets running nodes
    • number of nodes per wallet
    • whales running >5 nodes
    • casual users running 1 node
    • node churn (new nodes each week)
So you know EXACTLY:
    • total users running at least 1 node
    • how active they are
    • how node population grows over time

? 6. How do you know where nodes are physically located?
Using GEOIP.
When a node pings the API, the server logs:
    • IP ? city, region, country, continent
    • Approximate network provider
This is automatic, no user input required.
You never store private address, only region-level info for analytics.
This lets you build:
    • Maps
    • Distribution charts
    • Stats by region
    • Load balancing suggestions

? 7. What if 1000 people run nodes? Does it scale?
Yes — the Prime API design is extremely lightweight.
A GRID Node ping is ~1–2 KB.
1000 nodes pinging every 60 seconds =
1000–2000 KB/minute = ~1.5 MB/minute
? 90 MB per hour
? 2.1 GB per day
This is nothing.
A small VPS handles 10,000 nodes easily.

? 8. So here’s your answer in one sentence:
You know how many GRID Nodes exist, where they are, who runs them, and whether they are online because every node must register and ping the Prime API, which stores and displays all node data in a dashboard.
You will always see:
    • Exact node count
    • Regions
    • Online/offline status
    • Node owners
    • Activity per dApp
    • Uptime
    • Network health
    • Node version distribution
    • Reward eligibility
This system is the backbone of tracking.
